But it didn’t really go away. The low feeling and not really caring about things the way I used to — that’s been there for about that long. It’s hard because after a while it sort of becomes the background, so I almost forget when it started, but yeah… about two years and a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel really unlike myself.\n\nI used to be the person who would plan brunches with friends, like actually look up places and make reservations and get excited about seeing people. I used to paint in the spare room, not professionally or anything, but I liked having a little setup and spending a whole afternoon on it. I’d go hiking when the weather was nice, and I used to sign up for networking events for work because I felt like they were useful and kind of energizing, even if they were a little awkward sometimes.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… tasks. Like things I’m supposed to care about, or things I’m pretending I still care about because they sound like the kind of things a normal version of me would want to do. Even if I agree to something, I’m mostly thinking about how to get through it, not looking forward to it. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the pleasure got drained out of them, and I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t want to give you some exact number like I’ve been tracking it, because I really haven’t. But I’ve noticed my clothes fit differently — looser, mostly — and there have been times where I’ll put on pants that used to be snug and they’re just kind of hanging more than I remember.\n\nMy appetite has been really inconsistent, but most days it’s less that I’m intentionally not eating and more that I just… don’t manage real meals. I’ll have coffee, maybe toast, or something very easy, and then suddenly it’s late afternoon and I realize I haven’t eaten anything that actually counts as a meal. Then sometimes I’ll just grab whatever is easiest because I’m too tired to think about food.\n\nSo, yes, I think my weight has probably gone down some over the past couple of years, but it’s been gradual enough that I mostly noticed through my clothes and how little I’m eating during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience any pleasure when you're doing something you used to enjoy, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes maybe for a few seconds, but it’s not the same. Like, if I’m painting, there might be this tiny moment where I like a color or a brushstroke, and I think, okay, that looks nice. Or if I’m at brunch, I might laugh at something someone says. But it feels very brief, like it doesn’t really reach me all the way.\n\nMostly, the things I used to enjoy feel like tasks now. Planning brunches used to be fun — I’d look up menus and get excited about seeing people — and now it feels like something I’m forcing myself to do so I don’t seem like I’ve completely disappeared. Hiking used to clear my head, but now I’m often just thinking about how long until I can go home. Networking events used to feel productive, even energizing in a weird way, but now I feel like I’m acting interested more than actually being interested.\n\nSo… yes, maybe little flashes, but they don’t last. It’s more like I’m pretending to care about things I remember caring about.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're just going through the motions of daily life without any real sense of purpose or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s a good way to put it, actually. A lot of the time it feels like I’m just performing the outline of my life, if that makes sense. Like I remember what I used to do and who I used to be, so I keep trying to do those things, but there’s not much behind it.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends and actually enjoy picking the place, texting everyone, getting excited about it. I used to paint in the spare room for hours and lose track of time in a good way. I used to go hiking and feel like it gave me some kind of reset. Even networking events for work — which sounds kind of ridiculous — I used to sign up for them and feel like, okay, this is useful, this is part of building something.\n\nNow those things feel more like assignments. Like I’m pretending to care because I know I used to care. I’ll go to brunch and smile and talk, but inside I’m mostly just trying to get through it. I’ll sit in front of my paints and feel like I should be enjoying it, but it’s just… flat. Hiking feels like walking until it’s acceptable to go home. And work events feel like I’m playing the role of someone motivated.\n\nSo, yeah, I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot. It’s not that I’ve forgotten what enjoyment is supposed to look like. I just don’t really feel connected to it most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without these feelings of guilt, shame, and disconnection from enjoyable activities?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt completely back to normal. Not for any real stretch of time.\n\nThere have been times where I could function a little better, I guess — like I could get through work more cleanly, or I’d make plans and actually show up, or I’d have a day where I seemed more like myself from the outside. And sometimes that made me think, okay, maybe I’m coming out of it. But then underneath it, the heaviness and the guilt and this kind of distance from things were still there. It wasn’t like I suddenly felt connected again or genuinely excited about my life.\n\nLooking back over the past couple of years, it feels like the low mood and not really enjoying things have been there almost the whole time since it started. Maybe the intensity shifts — some weeks I’m more functional and some weeks I feel like I’m barely dragging myself through — but I can’t really point to a month or even a longer period where I’d say, yes, I was fully myself again and those feelings were gone.\n\nSo… brief better patches, yes. Completely normal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two years or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. Not exactly the same every single day, but I wouldn’t say it really comes and goes in a clean way.\n\nIt started about… two years and three months ago, around after I bought the townhouse. At first I thought it was just stress from that — like the pressure of keeping up with the mortgage and the repairs and just feeling like I had to prove I could handle it all. But then it didn’t really lift. It just sort of settled in and became the background of everything.\n\nThere are days or weeks where it feels less intense, where I can function better or seem more okay, but I don’t feel like it fully disappears. It’s more like the volume changes, not like it turns off. So yes, I’d say it’s been ongoing for a little over two years now, since the townhouse and all that pressure started building.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of continuous depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has been two years or more. When I try to put an actual timeline on it, the depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nIt’s strange saying it that precisely, because day to day it doesn’t feel like I’m counting it, but looking back, it’s been there continuously in some form. Some days are less heavy than others, but I don’t feel like there’s been a real break where I was just okay again. So, yes — about two years and one month.\n\nDoctor: Have your depressive feelings usually been present for most of the day, rather than just a small part of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it by hours in the day or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice it when I check in with myself, or when things get quiet, or when I’m trying to do something and realize I don’t feel much toward it. But I don’t know if I can confidently say, “yes, it’s most of the day,” versus “it’s there in the background and I become aware of it at different points.”\n\nSome days it feels like it colors the whole day, and other days I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking about it the whole time. So I guess I’d say it’s been present a lot, but I’m not sure I can accurately say how much of each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of significant improvement in your depressive symptoms that lasted for at least two months over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for two months at a time.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole stretch since this started. There have been little windows where I could function better, like I could get through work or take care of things and maybe seem more normal from the outside, but it didn’t feel like I was actually better for a sustained period.\n\nIt’s more like I’d have a few days, maybe a short patch, where it felt less heavy or I could push through more easily. But I can’t think of any solid two-month period where I’d say the depression really lifted or I felt back to myself. It’s been pretty consistently there underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Mostly hearing, not really seeing things.\n\nIt’s usually a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from somewhere real, like out in the hallway or sometimes through the air vent. That’s the part that makes it hard to dismiss, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just in my head in the moment. It sounds placed, like there’s actually someone there speaking.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all,” and when it happens I get really unsettled. I’ve actually gone outside or checked the hallway to see who was there, because it sounded that real to me. Then when I don’t find anyone, I feel embarrassed and scared, but also still not completely reassured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not completely free of it. I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where I felt like the depression was just gone.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the lack of interest in things have been there for almost the whole time since this started. There were times where I could function a little better, like I could get through the day, answer emails, do what I needed to do, maybe even seem okay if someone wasn’t looking too closely. But inside it didn’t feel like I was actually back to normal.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness got a little quieter for a while, or I was better at pushing through it. Not that I felt genuinely clear of it. So, no, I can’t point to a two-month stretch where I felt completely free of depressive symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a distinct episode of depression that started and stopped, rather than it being a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve tried to think about it that way, like whether there was a clear “episode” that began and then ended, but it doesn’t really feel like that’s how it’s been for me.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s more like a constant background thing than something that switched on and then switched off. There have been brief times where I could function a little better, like I could get through work or take care of basic things more easily, or maybe I seemed a bit more normal from the outside. But even then, it didn’t feel like the depression had actually stopped.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness was still there underneath, just maybe not as overwhelming every single moment. So I wouldn’t describe it as distinct episodes that started and stopped. Mostly hearing, not really seeing things.\n\nIt’s usually a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from somewhere real, like out in the hallway or sometimes through the air vent. That’s the part that makes it hard to dismiss, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just in my head in the moment. It sounds placed, like there’s actually someone there speaking.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all,” and when it happens I get really unsettled. I’ve actually gone outside or checked the hallway to see who was there, because it sounded that real to me. Then when I don’t find anyone, I feel embarrassed and scared, but also still not completely reassured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not completely free of it. I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where I felt like the depression was just gone.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the lack of interest in things have been there for almost the whole time since this started. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes, but not in a way where I’d say one week I’m depressed and then the next week I’m not. It’s more like… the volume changes, if that makes sense.

Looking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s kind of always sitting there underneath everything. Some weeks it’s heavier, and I feel like I’m dragging myself through even basic things. Other weeks I can function a bit better, like I can keep up with work tasks more, respond to people, get through the day without it being as obvious. But even during those better weeks, I don’t feel like it’s gone.

So yes, there are variations from week to week, but they feel more like small shifts in intensity rather than a real break from it. The depression is still there, just sometimes I’m better at pushing through it.

At home, too, I feel like I’m failing at being an adult, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve the things I’ve worked for. I keep thinking maybe I deserve to lose it all because I must have done something wrong or I’m not good enough to have it. It’s just this constant guilt and shame, and I can’t seem to talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly… or maybe I’m just not good at separating all of it. It’s more like this heavy feeling, like I’m worn down and disappointed in myself a lot of the time. Empty… sometimes, maybe, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed I don’t feel normal, if that makes sense. Like there’s this grayness over things, but I don’t always know whether that’s sadness or just being exhausted from thinking so much. It kind of comes in waves, but it also feels like it’s just there in the background most days. But it didn’t really go away. The low feeling and not really caring about things the way I used to — that’s been there for about that long. It’s hard because after a while it sort of becomes the background, so I almost forget when it started, but yeah… about two years and a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel really unlike myself.\n\nI used to be the person who would plan brunches with friends, like actually look up places and make reservations and get excited about seeing people. I used to paint in the spare room, not professionally or anything, but I liked having a little setup and spending a whole afternoon on it. I’d go hiking when the weather was nice, and I used to sign up for networking events for work because I felt like they were useful and kind of energizing, even if they were a little awkward sometimes.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… tasks. Like things I’m supposed to care about, or things I’m pretending I still care about because they sound like the kind of things a normal version of me would want to do. Even if I agree to something, I’m mostly thinking about how to get through it, not looking forward to it. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the pleasure got drained out of them, and I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t want to give you some exact number like I’ve been tracking it, because I really haven’t. But I’ve noticed my clothes fit differently — looser, mostly — and there have been times where I’ll put on pants that used to be snug and they’re just kind of hanging more than I remember.\n\nMy appetite has been really inconsistent, but most days it’s less that I’m intentionally not eating and more that I just… don’t manage real meals. I’ll have coffee, maybe toast, or something very easy, and then suddenly it’s late afternoon and I realize I haven’t eaten anything that actually counts as a meal. Then sometimes I’ll just grab whatever is easiest because I’m too tired to think about food.\n\nSo, yes, I think my weight has probably gone down some over the past couple of years, but it’s been gradual enough that I mostly noticed through my clothes and how little I’m eating during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience any pleasure when you're doing something you used to enjoy, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes maybe for a few seconds, but it’s not the same. Like, if I’m painting, there might be this tiny moment where I like a color or a brushstroke, and I think, okay, that looks nice. Or if I’m at brunch, I might laugh at something someone says. But it feels very brief, like it doesn’t really reach me all the way.\n\nMostly, the things I used to enjoy feel like tasks now. Planning brunches used to be fun — I’d look up menus and get excited about seeing people — and now it feels like something I’m forcing myself to do so I don’t seem like I’ve completely disappeared. Hiking used to clear my head, but now I’m often just thinking about how long until I can go home. Networking events used to feel productive, even energizing in a weird way, but now I feel like I’m acting interested more than actually being interested.\n\nSo… yes, maybe little flashes, but they don’t last. It’s more like I’m pretending to care about things I remember caring about.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're just going through the motions of daily life without any real sense of purpose or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s a good way to put it, actually. A lot of the time it feels like I’m just performing the outline of my life, if that makes sense. Like I remember what I used to do and who I used to be, so I keep trying to do those things, but there’s not much behind it.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends and actually enjoy picking the place, texting everyone, getting excited about it. I used to paint in the spare room for hours and lose track of time in a good way. I used to go hiking and feel like it gave me some kind of reset. Even networking events for work — which sounds kind of ridiculous — I used to sign up for them and feel like, okay, this is useful, this is part of building something.\n\nNow those things feel more like assignments. Like I’m pretending to care because I know I used to care. I’ll go to brunch and smile and talk, but inside I’m mostly just trying to get through it. I’ll sit in front of my paints and feel like I should be enjoying it, but it’s just… flat. Hiking feels like walking until it’s acceptable to go home. And work events feel like I’m playing the role of someone motivated.\n\nSo, yeah, I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot. It’s not that I’ve forgotten what enjoyment is supposed to look like. I just don’t really feel connected to it most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without these feelings of guilt, shame, and disconnection from enjoyable activities?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt completely back to normal. Not for any real stretch of time.\n\nThere have been times where I could function a little better, I guess — like I could get through work more cleanly, or I’d make plans and actually show up, or I’d have a day where I seemed more like myself from the outside. And sometimes that made me think, okay, maybe I’m coming out of it. But then underneath it, the heaviness and the guilt and this kind of distance from things were still there. It wasn’t like I suddenly felt connected again or genuinely excited about my life.\n\nLooking back over the past couple of years, it feels like the low mood and not really enjoying things have been there almost the whole time since it started. Maybe the intensity shifts — some weeks I’m more functional and some weeks I feel like I’m barely dragging myself through — but I can’t really point to a month or even a longer period where I’d say, yes, I was fully myself again and those feelings were gone.\n\nSo… brief better patches, yes. Completely normal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two years or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. Not exactly the same every single day, but I wouldn’t say it really comes and goes in a clean way.\n\nIt started about… two years and three months ago, around after I bought the townhouse. At first I thought it was just stress from that — like the pressure of keeping up with the mortgage and the repairs and just feeling like I had to prove I could handle it all. But then it didn’t really lift. It just sort of settled in and became the background of everything.\n\nThere are days or weeks where it feels less intense, where I can function better or seem more okay, but I don’t feel like it fully disappears. It’s more like the volume changes, not like it turns off. So yes, I’d say it’s been ongoing for a little over two years now, since the townhouse and all that pressure started building.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of continuous depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has been two years or more. When I try to put an actual timeline on it, the depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nIt’s strange saying it that precisely, because day to day it doesn’t feel like I’m counting it, but looking back, it’s been there continuously in some form. Some days are less heavy than others, but I don’t feel like there’s been a real break where I was just okay again. So, yes — about two years and one month.\n\nDoctor: Have your depressive feelings usually been present for most of the day, rather than just a small part of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it by hours in the day or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice it when I check in with myself, or when things get quiet, or when I’m trying to do something and realize I don’t feel much toward it. Maybe the intensity shifts — some weeks I’m more functional and some weeks I feel like I’m barely dragging myself through — but I can’t really point to a month or even a longer period where I’d say, yes, I was fully myself again and those feelings were gone.\n\nSo… brief better patches, yes. Completely normal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two years or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. Not exactly the same every single day, but I wouldn’t say it really comes and goes in a clean way.\n\nIt started about… two years and three months ago, around after I bought the townhouse. At first I thought it was just stress from that — like the pressure of keeping up with the mortgage and the repairs and just feeling like I had to prove I could handle it all. But then it didn’t really lift. It just sort of settled in and became the background of everything.\n\nThere are days or weeks where it feels less intense, where I can function better or seem more okay, but I don’t feel like it fully disappears. It’s more like the volume changes, not like it turns off. So yes, I’d say it’s been ongoing for a little over two years now, since the townhouse and all that pressure started building.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of continuous depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has been two years or more. When I try to put an actual timeline on it, the depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nIt’s strange saying it that precisely, because day to day it doesn’t feel like I’m counting it, but looking back, it’s been there continuously in some form. Some days are less heavy than others, but I don’t feel like there’s been a real break where I was just okay again. So, yes — about two years and one month.\n\nDoctor: Have your depressive feelings usually been present for most of the day, rather than just a small part of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it by hours in the day or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice it when I check in with myself, or when things get quiet, or when I’m trying to do something and realize I don’t feel much toward it. But I don’t know if I can confidently say, “yes, it’s most of the day,” versus “it’s there in the background and I become aware of it at different points.”\n\nSome days it feels like it colors the whole day, and other days I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking about it the whole time. So I guess I’d say it’s been present a lot, but I’m not sure I can accurately say how much of each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of significant improvement in your depressive symptoms that lasted for at least two months over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for two months at a time.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole stretch since this started. There have been little windows where I could function better, like I could get through work or take care of things and maybe seem more normal from the outside, but it didn’t feel like I was actually better for a sustained period.\n\nIt’s more like I’d have a few days, maybe a short patch, where it felt less heavy or I could push through more easily. But I can’t think of any solid two-month period where I’d say the depression really lifted or I felt back to myself. It’s been pretty consistently there underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Mostly hearing, not really seeing things.\n\nIt’s usually a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from somewhere real, like out in the hallway or sometimes through the air vent. That’s the part that makes it hard to dismiss, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just in my head in the moment. It sounds placed, like there’s actually someone there speaking.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all,” and when it happens I get really unsettled. I’ve actually gone outside or checked the hallway to see who was there, because it sounded that real to me. Then when I don’t find anyone, I feel embarrassed and scared, but also still not completely reassured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not completely free of it. I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where I felt like the depression was just gone.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the lack of interest in things have been there for almost the whole time since this started. There were times where I could function a little better, like I could get through the day, answer emails, do what I needed to do, maybe even seem okay if someone wasn’t looking too closely. But inside it didn’t feel like I was actually back to normal.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness got a little quieter for a while, or I was better at pushing through it. Not that I felt genuinely clear of it. So, no, I can’t point to a two-month stretch where I felt completely free of depressive symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a distinct episode of depression that started and stopped, rather than it being a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve tried to think about it that way, like whether there was a clear “episode” that began and then ended, but it doesn’t really feel like that’s how it’s been for me.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s more like a constant background thing than something that switched on and then switched off. There have been brief times where I could function a little better, like I could get through work or take care of basic things more easily, or maybe I seemed a bit more normal from the outside. But even then, it didn’t feel like the depression had actually stopped.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness was still there underneath, just maybe not as overwhelming every single moment. So I wouldn’t describe it as distinct episodes that started and stopped. It’s felt much more continuous, with only short stretches where I was a little more able to push through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant variations in how depressed you feel from one week to another?\n\nPatient: A little, yes, but not in a way where I’d say one week I’m depressed and then the next week I’m not. It’s more like… the volume changes, if that makes sense.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s kind of always sitting there underneath everything. Some weeks it’s heavier, and I feel like I’m dragging myself through even basic things. Other weeks I can function a bit better, like I can keep up with work tasks more, respond to people, get through the day without it being as obvious. But even during those better weeks, I don’t feel like it’s gone.\n\nSo yes, there are variations from week to week, but they feel more like small shifts in intensity rather than a real break from it. The depression is still there, just sometimes I’m better at pushing through it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes. Definitely more than two weeks.

Honestly, it’s been much longer than that. The depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF33.1, F31.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t keep going around in my head like this. Everything that goes wrong, even small things, I end up feeling like it’s my fault somehow. Like at work, I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, that I’m letting people down or I’m going to ruin something important, even when I’m trying really hard.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. At home, too, I feel like I’m failing at being an adult, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve the things I’ve worked for. I keep thinking maybe I deserve to lose it all because I must have done something wrong or I’m not good enough to have it. It’s just this constant guilt and shame, and I can’t seem to talk myself out of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly… or maybe I’m just not good at separating all of it. It’s more like this heavy feeling, like I’m worn down and disappointed in myself a lot of the time. Empty… sometimes, maybe, but I’m not sure if that’s the right word.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed I don’t feel normal, if that makes sense. Like there’s this grayness over things, but I don’t always know whether that’s sadness or just being exhausted from thinking so much. It kind of comes in waves, but it also feels like it’s just there in the background most days. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clean answer.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. Around two years and one month, if I’m trying to be accurate.\n\nI remember thinking at first it was just a rough patch, like maybe I was burned out or stressed, and that it would lift if I got through whatever was in front of me. But it didn’t really go away. The low feeling and not really caring about things the way I used to — that’s been there for about that long. It’s hard because after a while it sort of becomes the background, so I almost forget when it started, but yeah… about two years and a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually one of the things that makes me feel really unlike myself.\n\nI used to be the person who would plan brunches with friends, like actually look up places and make reservations and get excited about seeing people. I used to paint in the spare room, not professionally or anything, but I liked having a little setup and spending a whole afternoon on it. I’d go hiking when the weather was nice, and I used to sign up for networking events for work because I felt like they were useful and kind of energizing, even if they were a little awkward sometimes.\n\nNow all of that just feels like… tasks. Like things I’m supposed to care about, or things I’m pretending I still care about because they sound like the kind of things a normal version of me would want to do. Even if I agree to something, I’m mostly thinking about how to get through it, not looking forward to it. It’s not that I hate those things now, exactly. It’s more like the pleasure got drained out of them, and I’m just going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past two years?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself consistently, so I don’t want to give you some exact number like I’ve been tracking it, because I really haven’t. But I’ve noticed my clothes fit differently — looser, mostly — and there have been times where I’ll put on pants that used to be snug and they’re just kind of hanging more than I remember.\n\nMy appetite has been really inconsistent, but most days it’s less that I’m intentionally not eating and more that I just… don’t manage real meals. I’ll have coffee, maybe toast, or something very easy, and then suddenly it’s late afternoon and I realize I haven’t eaten anything that actually counts as a meal. Then sometimes I’ll just grab whatever is easiest because I’m too tired to think about food.\n\nSo, yes, I think my weight has probably gone down some over the past couple of years, but it’s been gradual enough that I mostly noticed through my clothes and how little I’m eating during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience any pleasure when you're doing something you used to enjoy, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes maybe for a few seconds, but it’s not the same. Like, if I’m painting, there might be this tiny moment where I like a color or a brushstroke, and I think, okay, that looks nice. Or if I’m at brunch, I might laugh at something someone says. But it feels very brief, like it doesn’t really reach me all the way.\n\nMostly, the things I used to enjoy feel like tasks now. Planning brunches used to be fun — I’d look up menus and get excited about seeing people — and now it feels like something I’m forcing myself to do so I don’t seem like I’ve completely disappeared. Hiking used to clear my head, but now I’m often just thinking about how long until I can go home. Networking events used to feel productive, even energizing in a weird way, but now I feel like I’m acting interested more than actually being interested.\n\nSo… yes, maybe little flashes, but they don’t last. It’s more like I’m pretending to care about things I remember caring about.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're just going through the motions of daily life without any real sense of purpose or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s a good way to put it, actually. A lot of the time it feels like I’m just performing the outline of my life, if that makes sense. Like I remember what I used to do and who I used to be, so I keep trying to do those things, but there’s not much behind it.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends and actually enjoy picking the place, texting everyone, getting excited about it. I used to paint in the spare room for hours and lose track of time in a good way. I used to go hiking and feel like it gave me some kind of reset. Even networking events for work — which sounds kind of ridiculous — I used to sign up for them and feel like, okay, this is useful, this is part of building something.\n\nNow those things feel more like assignments. Like I’m pretending to care because I know I used to care. I’ll go to brunch and smile and talk, but inside I’m mostly just trying to get through it. I’ll sit in front of my paints and feel like I should be enjoying it, but it’s just… flat. Hiking feels like walking until it’s acceptable to go home. And work events feel like I’m playing the role of someone motivated.\n\nSo, yeah, I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot. It’s not that I’ve forgotten what enjoyment is supposed to look like. I just don’t really feel connected to it most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without these feelings of guilt, shame, and disconnection from enjoyable activities?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt completely back to normal. Not for any real stretch of time.\n\nThere have been times where I could function a little better, I guess — like I could get through work more cleanly, or I’d make plans and actually show up, or I’d have a day where I seemed more like myself from the outside. And sometimes that made me think, okay, maybe I’m coming out of it. But then underneath it, the heaviness and the guilt and this kind of distance from things were still there. It wasn’t like I suddenly felt connected again or genuinely excited about my life.\n\nLooking back over the past couple of years, it feels like the low mood and not really enjoying things have been there almost the whole time since it started. Maybe the intensity shifts — some weeks I’m more functional and some weeks I feel like I’m barely dragging myself through — but I can’t really point to a month or even a longer period where I’d say, yes, I was fully myself again and those feelings were gone.\n\nSo… brief better patches, yes. Completely normal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two years or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. Not exactly the same every single day, but I wouldn’t say it really comes and goes in a clean way.\n\nIt started about… two years and three months ago, around after I bought the townhouse. At first I thought it was just stress from that — like the pressure of keeping up with the mortgage and the repairs and just feeling like I had to prove I could handle it all. But then it didn’t really lift. It just sort of settled in and became the background of everything.\n\nThere are days or weeks where it feels less intense, where I can function better or seem more okay, but I don’t feel like it fully disappears. It’s more like the volume changes, not like it turns off. So yes, I’d say it’s been ongoing for a little over two years now, since the townhouse and all that pressure started building.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of continuous depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has been two years or more. When I try to put an actual timeline on it, the depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nIt’s strange saying it that precisely, because day to day it doesn’t feel like I’m counting it, but looking back, it’s been there continuously in some form. Some days are less heavy than others, but I don’t feel like there’s been a real break where I was just okay again. So, yes — about two years and one month.\n\nDoctor: Have your depressive feelings usually been present for most of the day, rather than just a small part of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it by hours in the day or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice it when I check in with myself, or when things get quiet, or when I’m trying to do something and realize I don’t feel much toward it. But I don’t know if I can confidently say, “yes, it’s most of the day,” versus “it’s there in the background and I become aware of it at different points.”\n\nSome days it feels like it colors the whole day, and other days I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking about it the whole time. So I guess I’d say it’s been present a lot, but I’m not sure I can accurately say how much of each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of significant improvement in your depressive symptoms that lasted for at least two months over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for two months at a time.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole stretch since this started. There have been little windows where I could function better, like I could get through work or take care of things and maybe seem more normal from the outside, but it didn’t feel like I was actually better for a sustained period.\n\nIt’s more like I’d have a few days, maybe a short patch, where it felt less heavy or I could push through more easily. But I can’t think of any solid two-month period where I’d say the depression really lifted or I felt back to myself. It’s been pretty consistently there underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Mostly hearing, not really seeing things.\n\nIt’s usually a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from somewhere real, like out in the hallway or sometimes through the air vent. That’s the part that makes it hard to dismiss, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just in my head in the moment. It sounds placed, like there’s actually someone there speaking.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all,” and when it happens I get really unsettled. I’ve actually gone outside or checked the hallway to see who was there, because it sounded that real to me. Then when I don’t find anyone, I feel embarrassed and scared, but also still not completely reassured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not completely free of it. I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where I felt like the depression was just gone.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the lack of interest in things have been there for almost the whole time since this started. There were times where I could function a little better, like I could get through the day, answer emails, do what I needed to do, maybe even seem okay if someone wasn’t looking too closely. But inside it didn’t feel like I was actually back to normal.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness got a little quieter for a while, or I was better at pushing through it. Not that I felt genuinely clear of it. So, no, I can’t point to a two-month stretch where I felt completely free of depressive symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a distinct episode of depression that started and stopped, rather than it being a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve tried to think about it that way, like whether there was a clear “episode” that began and then ended, but it doesn’t really feel like that’s how it’s been for me.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s more like a constant background thing than something that switched on and then switched off. There have been brief times where I could function a little better, like I could get through work or take care of basic things more easily, or maybe I seemed a bit more normal from the outside. But even then, it didn’t feel like the depression had actually stopped.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness was still there underneath, just maybe not as overwhelming every single moment. So I wouldn’t describe it as distinct episodes that started and stopped. It’s felt much more continuous, with only short stretches where I was a little more able to push through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant variations in how depressed you feel from one week to another?\n\nPatient: A little, yes, but not in a way where I’d say one week I’m depressed and then the next week I’m not. It’s more like… the volume changes, if that makes sense.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s kind of always sitting there underneath everything. Some weeks it’s heavier, and I feel like I’m dragging myself through even basic things. Other weeks I can function a bit better, like I can keep up with work tasks more, respond to people, get through the day without it being as obvious. But even during those better weeks, I don’t feel like it’s gone.\n\nSo yes, there are variations from week to week, but they feel more like small shifts in intensity rather than a real break from it. The depression is still there, just sometimes I’m better at pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than two weeks.\n\nHonestly, it’s been much longer than that. The depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now. It’s hard to even separate it into smaller chunks, because it hasn’t felt like, “Oh, that was a two-week period,” and then it lifted. It’s been more like this ongoing thing that’s just stayed with me day after day.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had two weeks or more where I felt depressed most of the day nearly every day. I’d say that describes most of the past couple years, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think I’ve had anything close to eight months without significant depression symptoms.

Looking back over the past couple years, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time. There have been little stretches where I could function better, like I could get more done or keep up with things a bit more, and maybe from the outside I might have seemed like I was doing okay. But even during those times, it didn’t feel like the depression was actually gone.

It was more like I had a little more energy to push through it, or it wasn’t pressing down as hard every single day. But I can’t point to a long period, definitely not eight months, where I felt free of it or where those symptoms weren’t really there.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I really don’t think so. I’ve tried to think about that because it’s hard to track when it’s been going on this long, but I can’t remember a stretch of two full months where the depressive feelings were just… not there.

There have been brief periods where I could function a little better, like I could get through work, answer messages, do what I needed to do, and maybe I looked more okay from the outside. But the low mood and the loss of interest were still there underneath it. It wasn’t like I suddenly felt back to myself for a couple months.

So, no, not two months without symptoms. More like short times where it was a little easier to manage, but not actually gone.

It wasn’t like I suddenly felt connected again or genuinely excited about my life.\n\nLooking back over the past couple of years, it feels like the low mood and not really enjoying things have been there almost the whole time since it started. Maybe the intensity shifts — some weeks I’m more functional and some weeks I feel like I’m barely dragging myself through — but I can’t really point to a month or even a longer period where I’d say, yes, I was fully myself again and those feelings were gone.\n\nSo… brief better patches, yes. Completely normal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two years or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. Not exactly the same every single day, but I wouldn’t say it really comes and goes in a clean way.\n\nIt started about… two years and three months ago, around after I bought the townhouse. At first I thought it was just stress from that — like the pressure of keeping up with the mortgage and the repairs and just feeling like I had to prove I could handle it all. But then it didn’t really lift. It just sort of settled in and became the background of everything.\n\nThere are days or weeks where it feels less intense, where I can function better or seem more okay, but I don’t feel like it fully disappears. It’s more like the volume changes, not like it turns off. So yes, I’d say it’s been ongoing for a little over two years now, since the townhouse and all that pressure started building.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of continuous depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has been two years or more. When I try to put an actual timeline on it, the depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nIt’s strange saying it that precisely, because day to day it doesn’t feel like I’m counting it, but looking back, it’s been there continuously in some form. Some days are less heavy than others, but I don’t feel like there’s been a real break where I was just okay again. So, yes — about two years and one month.\n\nDoctor: Have your depressive feelings usually been present for most of the day, rather than just a small part of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it by hours in the day or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice it when I check in with myself, or when things get quiet, or when I’m trying to do something and realize I don’t feel much toward it. But I don’t know if I can confidently say, “yes, it’s most of the day,” versus “it’s there in the background and I become aware of it at different points.”\n\nSome days it feels like it colors the whole day, and other days I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking about it the whole time. Not for two months at a time.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole stretch since this started. There have been little windows where I could function better, like I could get through work or take care of things and maybe seem more normal from the outside, but it didn’t feel like I was actually better for a sustained period.\n\nIt’s more like I’d have a few days, maybe a short patch, where it felt less heavy or I could push through more easily. But I can’t think of any solid two-month period where I’d say the depression really lifted or I felt back to myself. It’s been pretty consistently there underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Mostly hearing, not really seeing things.\n\nIt’s usually a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from somewhere real, like out in the hallway or sometimes through the air vent. That’s the part that makes it hard to dismiss, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just in my head in the moment. It sounds placed, like there’s actually someone there speaking.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all,” and when it happens I get really unsettled. I’ve actually gone outside or checked the hallway to see who was there, because it sounded that real to me. Then when I don’t find anyone, I feel embarrassed and scared, but also still not completely reassured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not completely free of it. I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where I felt like the depression was just gone.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the lack of interest in things have been there for almost the whole time since this started. There were times where I could function a little better, like I could get through the day, answer emails, do what I needed to do, maybe even seem okay if someone wasn’t looking too closely. But inside it didn’t feel like I was actually back to normal.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness got a little quieter for a while, or I was better at pushing through it. Not that I felt genuinely clear of it. So, no, I can’t point to a two-month stretch where I felt completely free of depressive symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a distinct episode of depression that started and stopped, rather than it being a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve tried to think about it that way, like whether there was a clear “episode” that began and then ended, but it doesn’t really feel like that’s how it’s been for me.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s more like a constant background thing than something that switched on and then switched off. There have been brief times where I could function a little better, like I could get through work or take care of basic things more easily, or maybe I seemed a bit more normal from the outside. So I wouldn’t describe it as distinct episodes that started and stopped. It’s felt much more continuous, with only short stretches where I was a little more able to push through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant variations in how depressed you feel from one week to another?\n\nPatient: A little, yes, but not in a way where I’d say one week I’m depressed and then the next week I’m not. It’s more like… the volume changes, if that makes sense.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. But I’ve noticed my clothes fit differently — looser, mostly — and there have been times where I’ll put on pants that used to be snug and they’re just kind of hanging more than I remember.\n\nMy appetite has been really inconsistent, but most days it’s less that I’m intentionally not eating and more that I just… don’t manage real meals. I’ll have coffee, maybe toast, or something very easy, and then suddenly it’s late afternoon and I realize I haven’t eaten anything that actually counts as a meal. Then sometimes I’ll just grab whatever is easiest because I’m too tired to think about food.\n\nSo, yes, I think my weight has probably gone down some over the past couple of years, but it’s been gradual enough that I mostly noticed through my clothes and how little I’m eating during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you still experience any pleasure when you're doing something you used to enjoy, even if it's just for a moment?\n\nPatient: Sometimes maybe for a few seconds, but it’s not the same. Like, if I’m painting, there might be this tiny moment where I like a color or a brushstroke, and I think, okay, that looks nice. Or if I’m at brunch, I might laugh at something someone says. But it feels very brief, like it doesn’t really reach me all the way.\n\nMostly, the things I used to enjoy feel like tasks now. Planning brunches used to be fun — I’d look up menus and get excited about seeing people — and now it feels like something I’m forcing myself to do so I don’t seem like I’ve completely disappeared. Hiking used to clear my head, but now I’m often just thinking about how long until I can go home. Networking events used to feel productive, even energizing in a weird way, but now I feel like I’m acting interested more than actually being interested.\n\nSo… yes, maybe little flashes, but they don’t last. It’s more like I’m pretending to care about things I remember caring about.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're just going through the motions of daily life without any real sense of purpose or enjoyment?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s a good way to put it, actually. A lot of the time it feels like I’m just performing the outline of my life, if that makes sense. Like I remember what I used to do and who I used to be, so I keep trying to do those things, but there’s not much behind it.\n\nI used to plan brunches with friends and actually enjoy picking the place, texting everyone, getting excited about it. I used to paint in the spare room for hours and lose track of time in a good way. I used to go hiking and feel like it gave me some kind of reset. Even networking events for work — which sounds kind of ridiculous — I used to sign up for them and feel like, okay, this is useful, this is part of building something.\n\nNow those things feel more like assignments. Like I’m pretending to care because I know I used to care. I’ll go to brunch and smile and talk, but inside I’m mostly just trying to get through it. I’ll sit in front of my paints and feel like I should be enjoying it, but it’s just… flat. Hiking feels like walking until it’s acceptable to go home. And work events feel like I’m playing the role of someone motivated.\n\nSo, yeah, I do feel like I’m going through the motions a lot. It’s not that I’ve forgotten what enjoyment is supposed to look like. I just don’t really feel connected to it most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past two years where you felt completely back to normal, without these feelings of guilt, shame, and disconnection from enjoyable activities?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had a period where I felt completely back to normal. Not for any real stretch of time.\n\nThere have been times where I could function a little better, I guess — like I could get through work more cleanly, or I’d make plans and actually show up, or I’d have a day where I seemed more like myself from the outside. And sometimes that made me think, okay, maybe I’m coming out of it. But then underneath it, the heaviness and the guilt and this kind of distance from things were still there. It wasn’t like I suddenly felt connected again or genuinely excited about my life.\n\nLooking back over the past couple of years, it feels like the low mood and not really enjoying things have been there almost the whole time since it started. Maybe the intensity shifts — some weeks I’m more functional and some weeks I feel like I’m barely dragging myself through — but I can’t really point to a month or even a longer period where I’d say, yes, I was fully myself again and those feelings were gone.\n\nSo… brief better patches, yes. Completely normal, no.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two years or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: It’s been pretty continuous, I think. Not exactly the same every single day, but I wouldn’t say it really comes and goes in a clean way.\n\nIt started about… two years and three months ago, around after I bought the townhouse. At first I thought it was just stress from that — like the pressure of keeping up with the mortgage and the repairs and just feeling like I had to prove I could handle it all. But then it didn’t really lift. It just sort of settled in and became the background of everything.\n\nThere are days or weeks where it feels less intense, where I can function better or seem more okay, but I don’t feel like it fully disappears. It’s more like the volume changes, not like it turns off. So yes, I’d say it’s been ongoing for a little over two years now, since the townhouse and all that pressure started building.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of continuous depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it has been two years or more. When I try to put an actual timeline on it, the depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now.\n\nIt’s strange saying it that precisely, because day to day it doesn’t feel like I’m counting it, but looking back, it’s been there continuously in some form. Some days are less heavy than others, but I don’t feel like there’s been a real break where I was just okay again. So, yes — about two years and one month.\n\nDoctor: Have your depressive feelings usually been present for most of the day, rather than just a small part of it?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it by hours in the day or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… I notice it when I check in with myself, or when things get quiet, or when I’m trying to do something and realize I don’t feel much toward it. But I don’t know if I can confidently say, “yes, it’s most of the day,” versus “it’s there in the background and I become aware of it at different points.”\n\nSome days it feels like it colors the whole day, and other days I’m distracted enough that I’m not actively thinking about it the whole time. So I guess I’d say it’s been present a lot, but I’m not sure I can accurately say how much of each day.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of significant improvement in your depressive symptoms that lasted for at least two months over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not for two months at a time.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole stretch since this started. There have been little windows where I could function better, like I could get through work or take care of things and maybe seem more normal from the outside, but it didn’t feel like I was actually better for a sustained period.\n\nIt’s more like I’d have a few days, maybe a short patch, where it felt less heavy or I could push through more easily. But I can’t think of any solid two-month period where I’d say the depression really lifted or I felt back to myself. It’s been pretty consistently there underneath everything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. Mostly hearing, not really seeing things.\n\nIt’s usually a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from somewhere real, like out in the hallway or sometimes through the air vent. That’s the part that makes it hard to dismiss, because it doesn’t feel like it’s just in my head in the moment. It sounds placed, like there’s actually someone there speaking.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all,” and when it happens I get really unsettled. I’ve actually gone outside or checked the hallway to see who was there, because it sounded that real to me. Then when I don’t find anyone, I feel embarrassed and scared, but also still not completely reassured, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, not completely free of it. I don’t think I’ve had anything like a full two months where I felt like the depression was just gone.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the lack of interest in things have been there for almost the whole time since this started. There were times where I could function a little better, like I could get through the day, answer emails, do what I needed to do, maybe even seem okay if someone wasn’t looking too closely. But inside it didn’t feel like I was actually back to normal.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness got a little quieter for a while, or I was better at pushing through it. Not that I felt genuinely clear of it. So, no, I can’t point to a two-month stretch where I felt completely free of depressive symptoms.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were in a distinct episode of depression that started and stopped, rather than it being a continuous feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve tried to think about it that way, like whether there was a clear “episode” that began and then ended, but it doesn’t really feel like that’s how it’s been for me.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s more like a constant background thing than something that switched on and then switched off. There have been brief times where I could function a little better, like I could get through work or take care of basic things more easily, or maybe I seemed a bit more normal from the outside. But even then, it didn’t feel like the depression had actually stopped.\n\nIt was more like the heaviness was still there underneath, just maybe not as overwhelming every single moment. So I wouldn’t describe it as distinct episodes that started and stopped. It’s felt much more continuous, with only short stretches where I was a little more able to push through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant variations in how depressed you feel from one week to another?\n\nPatient: A little, yes, but not in a way where I’d say one week I’m depressed and then the next week I’m not. It’s more like… the volume changes, if that makes sense.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s kind of always sitting there underneath everything. Some weeks it’s heavier, and I feel like I’m dragging myself through even basic things. Other weeks I can function a bit better, like I can keep up with work tasks more, respond to people, get through the day without it being as obvious. But even during those better weeks, I don’t feel like it’s gone.\n\nSo yes, there are variations from week to week, but they feel more like small shifts in intensity rather than a real break from it. The depression is still there, just sometimes I’m better at pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than two weeks.\n\nHonestly, it’s been much longer than that. The depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now. It’s hard to even separate it into smaller chunks, because it hasn’t felt like, “Oh, that was a two-week period,” and then it lifted. It’s been more like this ongoing thing that’s just stayed with me day after day.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had two weeks or more where I felt depressed most of the day nearly every day. I’d say that describes most of the past couple years, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least eight months without any significant depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything close to eight months without significant depression symptoms.\n\nLooking back over the past couple years, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time. I’ve tried to think about that because it’s hard to track when it’s been going on this long, but I can’t remember a stretch of two full months where the depressive feelings were just… not there.\n\nThere have been brief periods where I could function a little better, like I could get through work, answer messages, do what I needed to do, and maybe I looked more okay from the outside. So I wouldn’t describe it as distinct episodes that started and stopped. It’s felt much more continuous, with only short stretches where I was a little more able to push through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant variations in how depressed you feel from one week to another?\n\nPatient: A little, yes, but not in a way where I’d say one week I’m depressed and then the next week I’m not. It’s more like… the volume changes, if that makes sense.\n\nLooking back, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time since this started. It’s kind of always sitting there underneath everything. Some weeks it’s heavier, and I feel like I’m dragging myself through even basic things. Other weeks I can function a bit better, like I can keep up with work tasks more, respond to people, get through the day without it being as obvious. But even during those better weeks, I don’t feel like it’s gone.\n\nSo yes, there are variations from week to week, but they feel more like small shifts in intensity rather than a real break from it. The depression is still there, just sometimes I’m better at pushing through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of two weeks or more where you felt depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely more than two weeks.\n\nHonestly, it’s been much longer than that. The depression and the loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month now. It’s hard to even separate it into smaller chunks, because it hasn’t felt like, “Oh, that was a two-week period,” and then it lifted. It’s been more like this ongoing thing that’s just stayed with me day after day.\n\nSo yes, I’ve had two weeks or more where I felt depressed most of the day nearly every day. I’d say that describes most of the past couple years, really.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least eight months without any significant depressive symptoms over the past two years?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had anything close to eight months without significant depression symptoms.\n\nLooking back over the past couple years, the low mood and the loss of interest have been there for almost the whole time. There have been little stretches where I could function better, like I could get more done or keep up with things a bit more, and maybe from the outside I might have seemed like I was doing okay. But even during those times, it didn’t feel like the depression was actually gone.\n\nIt was more like I had a little more energy to push through it, or it wasn’t pressing down as hard every single day. But I can’t point to a long period, definitely not eight months, where I felt free of it or where those symptoms weren’t really there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you did not experience any depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, I really don’t think so. I’ve tried to think about that because it’s hard to track when it’s been going on this long, but I can’t remember a stretch of two full months where the depressive feelings were just… not there.\n\nThere have been brief periods where I could function a little better, like I could get through work, answer messages, do what I needed to do, and maybe I looked more okay from the outside. But the low mood and the loss of interest were still there underneath it. It wasn’t like I suddenly felt back to myself for a couple months.\n\nSo, no, not two months without symptoms. More like short times where it was a little easier to manage, but not actually gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of significant elevation in mood or increased energy that lasted for several days or more over the past two years?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I don’t think I’ve had anything that I would describe as a clear, significant elevation in mood. Like, I’ve had days where maybe I got more done or felt a little less weighed down, but I don’t know if that counts.\n\nWhen you say “increased energy,” I’m trying to think if there were stretches of several days where I was noticeably more energized in a way that stood out. I can’t really point to something like that. Mostly, if I had better days, it felt more like I was functioning a bit better than usual, not like my mood was unusually high or anything.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… I haven’t noticed any real periods like that, at least not that I can clearly identify."
